S.O. 673.—In exercise of the powers conferred by section 
17 of the Export (.Quality Control and Inspection) Act, 1963, 
(22 of 1963), the Central Government hereby makes the 
following rules further to amend the rules specified in the 
Annexure annexed heretb, namely :— 

1. In the 26 rules specified in the Annexure, for rule 6, 
the following shall be substituted, namely :-=r 

“6 Inspection fee :—Inspection fee shall be paid by the 
exportei to the Agency as under :— 

(i) (a) for exports under in-process quality control 
scheme at the rate of 0,2% of the F.O.B. value 
subiect to a minimum of Rs, 50/- per consign¬ 
ment ; 

(b) for exports under consignment wise inspection at 
the rate of 0.4% of the F.O.B. value subject to 
a minimum of Rs. 50/- per consignment. 

(i) subject to a minimum of Rs. 50/- per consigiment. 
the lates shall be 0.18 per cent and 0.36 per cent 
for sub-clause (a), and (b) respectively for manu- 
factiirers-exporters who are registered as small scale 
manufacturing units with the concerned Govern¬ 
ments of State/Union Territories.

New Delhi, the 8th Febriiaiy, 1991 

S.O. 687.—Whereus it rpjiefir to the Central Government 
that it is necessary in the public interest that for the transport 
of petioleiim from MORI-I to G.C.S, NAGARAM in A.P. 
State Pipe line .should be laid by the Oil & Natural Gas 
Commission. 

And wheieas it appear that for the purpose of layinc such 
pipeline, it is necessary to acquire that right of user in the 
land desciibed in the schedule annexed hereto: 

Now. therefore, in exercise of the powers conferied by 
sub-section (I) of the section 3 of the Petroleum and Mine¬ 


rals pipelines (Acquisition of Right of User in the land) 
Act, 1962 the Central Government hereby declare its inten¬ 
tion to acquire the right of user therein. 

Provided that any person interested in the said land may, 
within 21 days from the date of this Notification, object to 
the laying of the pipeline under the land to the Competent 
Authority, Oil & Natural Gas Commission, Construction & 
Maintenance Division, K.O. Projccf. Rnjahmundiy, (5.33103). 

/\nd every person makinc such an objection .‘■ball also stale 
sftcifically where he wishes lo be hear m pci son or by legal 
practitioner.

to the Government. 

And, further, whereas the Central Government has, after 
considerating the said report decided to acquire the Right of

acquired for laying the Gas pipeline.

New Delhi, the 19th February, 1991 

S.O. 709.—In exercise of the powers conferred by sub. 
section (1; of Section 3 of the Cinematograph Act, 1952 
(37 of 1952), read with rule 3 of the Cinematograph (Certi¬ 
fication) Rules, 1983 and in supersession of earlier Notifica¬ 
tions on the subject, the Central Government is pleased to 
reconstitute the Central Board of Film Certification and 
appoint the following persons as members of the said Board 
with immediate effect until further orders ;— 

1. Shri Shakti Samanta 

2. Shri Mamnohan Shetty 

3. Shri Rakesh Sinha 

4. Smt. Pranayani Munshi 

5. Ms. Dipti Naval 

6. Shri Biifiu Bhattacharya 

7. Smt. Sarojini Muthiah 

8. Shri A. V, Balasubramaniam 

9. Shri Gemini Gansesan 

10. Shri V. R. Venkatachalam 

11. Smt. Nandita Krishna 

12. Shri Ajoy De 

13. Shri P. Sengupta 

14. Shri Harsh Kumar Neotia 

15. Smt. Jeevaraj Alva 

16. Smt. N. Kaleeswaran 

17. Shri P. K. Padman 

18. Shri T. Subbarami Reddy 

19. Shri J. Bhaskara Rao 

20. Shri Amarendra Saran 

21. Shri Raj Bisaria 

22. Smt. Sara Mohammed 

23. Smt. Kamala Mankckar 

24. Shri Diljit Singh Pannun 

25. Smt. Urvashi Talwar. 

2 In exercise of the aforesaid powers, the Cenrtal Gov¬ 
ernment alsoi directs that with the reconstitution of the 


1209 

Central Board of Film Certification as mentioned above, the 
existing members (except S. Nos. 1, 2, 8, 9, 12, 18 and 
23)’ shall cease to be members of the Board with immediate 
effect.

The Central Government by their Order No. E-29012/33'' 
89TR (Misc.) dated 2.1-S-1989 have rcfcjred the lollowing

“Whether the action ol the management of M/s, Pandu¬ 
ranga Timblo Industries. Margao hi terminating the 
services of Smt. Amruti Naik, Cook at Talaulim 
Mines w.e.L Oc,ober. 1988 rs justified If not, 
what relief is the said workmen entitled to 7” 

2. The Joint Secretary of the Goa Mining Labour 'Wel¬ 
fare Union filed the statement of clam (Ex. 2) challenging 
the said action of the manugement. 

3. Thereafter while the reference was at the stage of filing 
written statement by the management, the partner of the 
management hied an application (Ex. 3) stating that the 
matter is already mutually settled between them and the 
workman, and that an amount of Rs. 12,635 has already 
been paid to the lady in question i.e. Smt. Amrutem Na'k 
by Cheque No. 0853919 dated 4-1-1991, and as such the 
matter may be disposed of. The representative of the Union 
also made an endorsement below [hat application that the 
matter is settled and as such it be disposed ot. The manage¬ 
ment also produced zerox copy of the receipt (Ex. 4) passed 
by the said lady in favour of the management. The said 
lady clearly stated in that document that she has received 
the amount of Rs. 12,635 in full and final settlement of all 
legal dues in terms of the mutual settlenient and that she 
has no further claim of whatsoever nature against the 
management, and that she intends to withdraw the present 
case pending before this Tiibunal. Therefore, as the matter 
is amicably settled as above, the present reference stands 
disposed erf,

REASONS 

3. The woikman Slin R. f.r. Oaikwad filed his affidavit

worknoan. By the letter dated 27-6-1986 (Ex. 12) the

cause of your aforesaid persistent lapses, unauthorised, 
habitual absence, derelict'cn in duties, misappropriation

to collect your dues from the Accouuiani of the Company, 
as early as possible”. The sa’d management witness referred

The workman Shri R. G Gaikwad was employed in

in duties, misappnioriafion of money etc., some

He w'ithheld 9 Way Bilks pertaining to Voltas Ltd. for

asked to explain but he did not submit his ex¬

27-12-1978. He frequently absented himself from

frequently availed excess leave when not due.

attendance etc. the company management lost con¬

to dispense with his service as discharge s-mplicitor 
from 1-7-1986.” 

5. As such, according to the management, the termination 
of service of the said workman was only discharge _ simpli- 
citor w'thout any punishment. Even then I find that the 
said termination of the service of the workman falls within 
the definition of terms ‘rstienchinent’. Under Section 2(oo) 
of the Industrial Disputes Act ‘retrenchment’ means the 
termination by the employer oi the servee of a workman 
for any reason whatsoever, otherwise than as a punishment 
inflicted by way of disciplinary action. Therefore, the termi¬ 
nation of service of the workman was letrenchment from 
service by the management, eventhough h is styled as dis¬ 
charge simpJicitor by the management. In a recent case it 
has been held by the Supreme Court reported in Current 
Labour Reports (1990-Vol. II) (Tlie Punjab Land Develop¬ 
ment and Reclamation Corporation Ltd, \'s. The Presiding 
Officer, Labour Court, Chandigarh and othens), the retrench¬ 
ment means the termination by ilie employer of the service 
of a workman for any reason whatsoever except those ex¬ 
pressly excluded m Section 2(oo) of the Industrial Disputes 
Act. As the teminaticn of the services of the workman 
was retrenchment from jervics, ffip provisions of Section 25-F 
of the Industrial Disputes Act should have been complied. 
Tn this case the provisions of Section 25-1- have not been 
conipVed with. Under Section 25-F of the Act. no work¬ 
man emnloyed in anv industry who has been in continuous 
service for no* less than one year under an employer shall 
bp rcfrenclied bv that employer until (a) the workman has 
b-en gVen one month’s notice in writing indicating the 
rassons for retrenchm.'-nt and the permd of notice has ex- 
ni|pd. or the workman tvs been paid in lieu of such notice, 
wages for the period of notice (b) the workman has been 



1212 THE GAZETTE OF INDIA : MARCH 9, 1991/PHALGUNA 18, 1912 [Part II—Sec. 3{li)] 


paid at the time of retienchineiu, coupensalioa which tihall 
tie equivalent to fitteeii Ua>a' avtiuyc pay for every com- 
plctcu year of continuotis '■■.erxice oi any pan thereof m 

excess of six months .' AdinitteUly, no letrenchracnt 

compensation was given b> the niunagomcm nor he lias been 
paid one months wages m licu ot notice, i uithei, admit¬ 
tedly he was in the sei'ite of the company loi 8 years 
from 1978 to 1986. fheietoic. the said lemunatton of 
service of the workman by tlie munagtment is not just, 
val'd and legal. As such the woikman in question will be 
entitled to leinstaiement i,i sci,ue wun lull back wages and 
continuity of service. In the pic.seut case only thice days’ 
notice was given by the management to the workman before 
his services weie icimiiiated with etlev,t Ijoni 1-7-1986 b\ 
Che letter dated 27-6-1986 (Ex. 15). Accordng to the woik¬ 
man, his dismissal from service by the management was by 
way of punishment, and it was not a discharge simnlicitor. 
Now, m case it is held that the said d sniissal was by way 
of punishment, then it is an admitted fact that or uo domes¬ 
tic enquiry wa.s held agaitia!: the woikman for any of the 
charge and as such no chaige was proved against him beioic 
bis serv'ces were terminated by way of pviinshmcnt. In that 
case also, the workman wiU be entitled to reinstatement in 
service with full back wages and continuity of service. 

6, The said management wimcss stated in his afllduv't 
that after,his discliaigo from the said company the woikman 
was employed as Dock Clerk m the establishment of O. V. 
Pednekar, Custom HoiiiC and Clearing Agents located at 
Devchayo, Jarasciji Dadaji Road, Bombay-400034, How¬ 
ever, the workman stated in his aflidavh that since his dis¬ 
missal from service, he is unemployed all the while. Now, 
in case the workman was to be m scivicc at at Bombay 
then he would have mentioned the place of bis residence 
as Bombay, when his evidence was lecoidcd n the Court. 
However, he stated his addicss as Painei, Dist Ahmednagar. 
Therefore. I find that since the date of his teiminntion of 
service he is unemployerl, and as such be is entitled to 
full back wages, 

7, f find that the termination of serv'ce of the workman 
was not by way of punkshment, but it was only discharge 
simplicitor which araomued to retrenchment in the present 
case. Further, he was gii'en sufficient opportunity to defend 
himself when sevcial Metros were ''ssued to him. Theicfore. 
Issues Nos. 1, 2. 4, 5 and 6 arc found as mentioned above. 

ISSUE No, 7 

8, The following Award is, therefore, passed. 

AtV.NRD 

(i) The action of tbs management of M/s. Sylvester 
and Co., Custom House Clearing and T orwarding 
Agents, operating in the roajo" port of Bombay in

the services of Shri R. G. Giiikwad, Dock Clerk 
w.e.f. 1-7-1986 is not iustified. 

(ii) The said management is hereby directed to reinstate

tinuity of service immediately. 

(iii) The parties to bear their own costs of this reference.

3. The union contends tliat withuut assigning any reason 
the servicc.s of the fifteen workers were teimiiialed on

v/hrle they were working, they were not given any benefit

court for gelling some of the monelai’y benefits. These 
female Khalasies while working along with other Male 
Khalasies, were doing all kinds of work. Since they were 
having more number of years of service they were entitled 
for rcgularlsation in .service or in the alternative they arc 
cnt’ticd for terminal benefits in accordance with the provi¬ 
sions contained in the 1. D. Act, Moreover, if their services 
were not term'natcd, they would h.we been screened for 
rcgulurisation in service also. 

4. In the counter statement filed by the Railways it is 
stated that all these workers concerned were engaged purely 
on casual bas's. They were employed only for short dura¬ 
tions. They were called fet work depending upon the exi¬ 
gencies of the work. None of them had continuous service 
either for one year or for 240 days during the period of 
12 calendar months proeeeTing their date of termination 
for enabling them to receive any kind of tc.minal benefits. 
From the very beginning of their engagements, they vvere 
not having continuous service and their services were mostly 
irregular' and there were intermittent bi'caks also, The- 
engagement of these casual labourers was of casual in nature 
and the work assigned to these workers were not at all rclat- 
mg to renewal of railway tracks Even among the male 
Khalasies or Men Mazdoors, there were different categories. 
The services of none of ihc workers concerned were required 
then because no work of casual nature was available. When 
these worjeers wei'e employed on ca.sual basis they were 
not paid Ihe CPC scale. Tb':y would be entitled for tem¬ 
porary status and CPC scale of pay only if they vvould 
complete 180/120 days of coniinuous work without break. 
Since none of the workers did not have that much serv'ce, 
they were not entitled for any terminal benefits available 
to workers having temporary status. Regarding the conten¬ 
tion of the union that while terminating the services of these 
woi ker« Ihe Railway liii'l not compi 'ed with the provision 
contained in the I. D, Act, the contention of the Railways 
is that since none of them have service qualifications as 
contemplated in Section 2'i-B (2) of the 1 D. Act there 
was no need for complying with the formal'lies contempla¬ 
ted in Section 25-F of the I D. Act. According to them, 
for almost identical relief these workers had approached the 
High Court of Kerala earlier by filing O.P. No, 4178/80 
and that writ petition vvas dismissed. However, in writ 
appeal while dismissing the writ petition the Division Bench 
of the High Court opined fin the judgment in WA No. 613/ 
83) that the workers heiei,i should seek remedy provided in 
the Industrial Dispute Act. 

5. It is a fact that challenging the terminations and seek¬ 
ing declaration for their continuance m H-rvicc, these workers 
had approached the High Court by filing writ petition. 
The Division Bench of the High Court has observed that 
the question whether each woiker had completed 240 days 
or not is a matter concerning of facts and under Article 
226 of the constitution such matter’s cannot bo adjudicaterl. 
The High Court has .iLo ohser''ed that the appropriate 
remedy for the workers is to invoke the pi'ovisions of the 
Industrial Dispute Act. In the light of that observation, 
the iin’on mo'vcd the labour Department of the Govcin- 
ment of India and nllimately the matter was referred foi 
adjudication to this Tribunal. 

6. On the "ide of the union one of the workers con¬ 
cerned as also the Secrei.nry of the union were c.xomined. 
On the side of the mrnagement a Permanent Way Inspector 
attached to Palghat Division was also cxam'ned.

7. While perusing tire refcretici order it can be seen that 
the only question to be decided is the validity of the ternii- 
nation effected in the case ol the woikcrs concerned in the 
dispute. The union's case ii that these workers were hav¬ 
ing length of service va'ying from four years to 14 years. 
They arc having continuous service as contemplated in Sec¬ 
tion 2.'!-B of the T. O. Act. ID view of that the termina¬ 
tions in their case were nothing but letrenchmcnt and in 
that case then terminations should have licen only in accor¬ 
dance with Section 25 0 of the I. D. Act. Other contention 
raised in that in terms of Section 25-H of the 1. D. Act, 
they me entitled for p'efe’cnti.il right in the matter cf 
employment also. Tt is also the case of the union that a 
casual labouicr under raihv.iys will attain the temporary 
status on completion of 120 days of continuous service. 
To vindicate that pos t'on, union relies on Railway Manual 
(Chapter 251- .According to them a casual labourer will 
attain temporary status if he'she has got 120 days of service 
during a period of six months. 

8. Now let us exauiine the claim^ of the union. In the 
course of proceeding, the union filed a pet'tion causing 
production of some of the documents which vmiild show 
the service particulars of the workers concerned. They 
sought the production of original pay sheets, muster roll, 
service register (LH Register), and service cards. The 
plea of the union on that behalf was resisted by the 
management through an affidavit stating that aU the 
original pay sheets were destroyed on expiry of the period 
specified for the presenaition of documenf.s and that was 
in terms of Rule 2004 and 2005 of Indian Railway General 
Code. Regarding the service cards it is stated' by the 
employer railways that those service cards should be only 
in the custody of the workers concerned, Tn relation to 
muster sheets it is stated that smcfc preservation period is 
already Over, those aie not kept in the q||ce. However 
they could produce muster sheets in relation to six of the 
casual labourers. They had produced the photo copies of 
the LTI register relating to these workers, 

9. Ext. W-1 scries are the photo copies of the service 
cards in respect of the workers concerned. The entries 
n Ext. W-1 series regarding the service particulars are tally¬ 
ing with ihe service particulars maintained by t.ic Railways 
after verifying LTI register. Since the particulais contained 
in Ext. W-1 series are not varying with the service particulars 
furnished by the Railways the entries in W-1 series can 
be taken into account to make an assessment oi the service 
details of each woiker. It is a fact that none of them 
are h.aving 240 days of continuous service during a perod 
of 12 calendar months immediately preceding the date of 
termination. If we look at the scivice particulars it can 
be seen that during the in tial period, these workers had 
worked almost all the day.s. After engaging them for about 
200 days, there would be a gap extending to one month or 
two months. Because of that break, none of them would 
be eligible for making claim for 240 days of service. But 
while taking into account the total service rendered by each, 
it can be seen that they were having more than 240 days of 
.service to their credit, but only thing is that those services 
were not at nil continuous. 

10. Tt is admitted in the counter statement TiIclI by the 
Railways that these casual workers were employed on the 
basis of work .sanctions given by the Railway administra¬ 
tion. It appears that although the requiremints of these 

workers were there to continue the work. Sanction were 
474 Gl/91—14 


being given in such a manner that there would be some 
kind ol break in the seiviccs of the workers concerned. 
Now the question is whcthei such a break can be included 
in the exclusion clause provided in Section 25-B of the 
1. D. Act. 1 he union contends that such a break was not 
at all on account of any faidt on the part of the workers. 
The delay in getting sanction was the real reason for it. 
If that position ’s accepted all these workers will have con- 
linuous service of more than 240 days so as to make them 
eligible to claim terminal benefits in accordance with the 
provisions contained in Sect’on 25-F of the I. D, Act. 

11. In the couisc of evidence it is revealed that so far 
.IS casual employment in Railways is concerned if a worker 
works continuously for 120 days, he would get temporary 
status. If the person having such temporary status is to 
he retrenched, he should be g'ven 14 days’ notice in terms 
of railways establishment mamial chapter XXIII. If we 
go through Ext. W-1 series of service cards (photo copies) 
it can he seen that all these workers were having more than 
120 days of service at the time of their termination, of 
course, with intenn’ttant breaks. This position is not dis¬ 
puted by the Railways, WW-1 Fathimabeevi had rendered 
service to the Railways considerably for a long period i.e., 
from 1973 to 1980. She got casual employment for three 
days to thirty days during a period of one month, 

12. Tn the counter statement filed by the management 
themselves it 's admitted that casual employees are entitled 
for temporary status and CPC scale of pay if they complete 
180''120 days of continuous service. But in the same breath 
in paragraph 7 of the counter statement they say that a 
person holding a post without a lien in the permanent post 
is not entitled to temporary status. Relying on a provision 
relaring to temporary railway servant, it is contended by 
the Railways that even for getting temporary status to a 
casual labourer there must be a lien for him in a permanent 
post. This assumption is ab.solutely irrelevant for a situation 
now Under discussion. 


13. Section 25-F of the T, D. Act provides that a person 
who has been in continuous service for not less than one 
year under an employer shall be retrenched only by giving 
one month's notice in writing, giving reason for the retrench¬ 
ment or in lieu of such notice one month’s wages. It is 
also provided therein that While effecting such retrenchment 
compensation in terms of Section 25-F (b) should also be 
given. The service qualification viz., ‘not less than one year’ 
's explained in Section 25-B wherein it is stipulated that 
for the purpose of continuous service for one year a person 
need not work for one full year. It will be sufficient that 
he needs work only 240 days at any time during a period of 
12 calendar month’s immediately preceding the date of 
retrenchment. Evidently, in this dispute, all the workers 
concerned; had been under employment for more than one 
year, of course, whh Intermittent breaks in their service. 
Their length of service varies from four years to 14 years. 
Since Ihe workers concerrle'd were having service spread 
over for a period of more than one year at the time of 
I heir ‘termination, it Is not necessary that they should satisfy 
the other alternative condiiton that they should have worked 
for 240 days continuously during a period of 12 calendar 
months. If vro analyse the true legislative intend, it can 
only mean that the insistance of 240 days is relevant for 
a situation where a worker could not work all the 12 
calendar months immediately preceding the date of retrench¬ 
ment. That is more evident if we look at the position 
that during the period of one year even if there is a break 
of three months continuously, the worker will be entitled 
for the benefits contemplated fn Action 25-F of the I. D, 
Act. if he had at least 240 days of service to his credit at 
any time during a period spread over from eight to twelve 
months. If that he the position, the case of workers who 
had rendered service for several years stands at a different 
footing. No mln’mum continuous service of 240 days need 
not he insisted In their case because they had rendered more 
number of years of service. True, that is with Inteimittant 
breaks. Since ail the workers concerned are having more 
than one year’s of service, they are entitled for benefits 
as provided under Section 2.5-F of the I. D, Act. 'TTius. 
nnv kind of tenninntion effected in their case not In com- 
plirnce of Sect’on 25-F of f. D, Act can only be declared 
ns illegal and void. 



1216 


THE GAZETTE OF INDIA ; MARCH % 1991/PHALGUNA 18, 1912 [Part II— Sec. 3(ii)] 


14. Viewing the entire aspect through another compass, 
these employees are governed by Railway Establishment 
Manual also. A casual worker who has rendered 120 days 
of continuous service Juung a pejiod of six months is 
entitled for ‘temporary status’. The evidence adduced by 
the union is to the effect that a worker who satisfies the 
condition relating to 120 days of service within a period of 
six months could be retrenched only after giving 14 days 
of notice or in lieu of that wages for that period. The 
Railways may say that since the workers concerned in this 
dispute do not saitsfy the service q^liflcations for getting 
even the temporary status, any reliance on the provision 
relaf’ng to retrenchment of persons having temporary status 
is of no relevance. If we go throuj^ the service cards 
Ext. W-1 series it can be seen that breakages in the service 
of these workers were effected iti such a manner that their 
service shall not be continuous even for making claim for 
temporary status. After putting break to their serv'ces, 
they were called again to continue the same-types of works. 
Explanation for it is given by the Railways saying that they 
could cari 7 out the work only on the basis of sanction 
orders which would be contained from t’rae to time from 
the Railway administration. Since the artificial break effec- • 
ted in the services of the workers herein is found to be not 
on account of any fault on their part, it can only be held 
that all of them satisfied the requirements even for conferr¬ 
ing the temporary status. Evidently, while terminating tlpplr 
services, the Railway had not compiled with the provls'ons 
confined in the Railway Establishment Manual also. 

15. In view of the fact that the terminations were found 
to be illegal it Is unnecessary to make any pronommeement 
on the questionability of the pleas made by the union relying 
on Section 25-G and H of the I, D. Act. In view of my 
declaration that the terminations effected In the case of 
these workers are illegal, they are entitled for reinstatement 
with back wages. For the purpose of calculation of thdr 
back wages, the total earning they had during the last 12 
months immediately before the termination will be the wages 
for one year. For calculating the total wages, the fraction 
of any year less than half need not be taken Into account. 
T<1I these works are validly terminated either in terms of 

I. D. Act or In terms of Railway Establishment Manual, 
they are deemed to continue in service. 

Award is passed in the above terms.

2. The case of the sponsoring union, Bihar Colliery Kamgai 
Union,' as disclosed in the written statement submitted by it 
on behalf of the concerned workmen, details apart, is aa 
follows: 

S/Shri Panchanan flouii and other concerned workmen 
have been working as permanent liuck Loaders within the 
precinct and piemises oi the Kuiya Colliery of Mys, B.C.C. 
i-td. since lung with unblemished record of service. They 
have been perfoimmg the job of coal loader under the direct 
control and supervision of the management of Kuiya Colliery. 
All the implements necessary for eAeculion of the job are 
being supplied by the management to the concerned workmen. 
Lach of the concerned workmen has put in more than 240 
days attendance in each calendar year as coal loader. The 
job of loadeis is a prohibited category of job. M/s. B.C.C. 
Ltd. has got permanent employees who have been doing the 
same nature of job of loading of coal at the same place 
along with the concerned wprkmcn. Despite these facts the 
management has been disbursing the wages of the concerned 
workmen through intermediary and that too below the rates of 
Wage Board Recommendation and N.C.W.A. I, II, JUI and 
IV. The union served a strike notree on 29-12-1981 demand¬ 
ing tegularisalion of the concerned workmen and payment 
of wages at par with other permanent loaders. The cqncilin- 
tion proceeduig over the matter ended in a failure due to 
the adamant attitude of the management. The then Govern¬ 
ment was known for its anti-labour attitude and policy; it 
did not either refer the dispute for adjudication nor did it 
reject the dispute for adjudication and kept It pending since 
1982. The union and the workmen concerned represented 
to the Ministry and the ditferent authorities for earljer refer¬ 
ence of the dispute for adjudication, but without any effect. 
Seeing no othei alternaitvc the union filed representation 
before Hon’ble High Court at Patna, Ranchi Bench, which 
was legistered as C.W.J.C. No. 1330/83(R). The Hon’ble 
Court was pleased to direct the Government to pass proper 
order in accordance with law. Thereupon, the Central Gov¬ 
ernment, appreciating the legal position, referred the dispute 
for adjudication. The demand of the workmen of Kuiya 
Colliery for legularisation of the concerned workmen lor 
treating them as departmental workmen are legal and justified. 
The action of the management in not regularising the con¬ 
cerned workmen and not treating them a-s depertmental em¬ 
ployees are illegal, arbitrary, unjustified and against the prin- 
ciplea of natural justice. The management Las not been 
paying them "wages as per Wage Board Recommendation and 
N.C.W.A. L U, in and IV. The action of the management 
is vindictive, discriminatory and smacks of anti-labour policy, 
•The management had not procured any registration for engag¬ 
ing the concerned workmen as contractor’s workmen nor 
had the alleged contractor procured any licence for engaging 
the concerned workmen in the job of coal loading, Disburse¬ 
ment of wages through different intermediaries is nothing 
but a legal camouflage. In the circumstances, the union has 
demanded that the concerned workmen be regtdarised as 
departmental workmen with retrospective effect and be paid 
Wages as r^r N.C.W.As. The union has further submitted 
that some inaccuracies have crept in the Annexuro to the 
reference containing the names of some of the concerned 
workmen and it has submitted the correct names with n 
prayer for acceptance of the same. 

3. Despite notices issued under registered post from tfio 
office of the Tribunal calling upon the management to appear 
and to make submission on the demand of the sponsoring

to contest the claim.

5. The case of the sponsoring union is that the concerned 
workmen have been working as Truck Loaders of coal In

8 Upon consideration of these evidence on lecord, 1 come 
to the inescapable conclusion that the concerned workmen 
have been working in Kuiya Colliery of M/s BCC Ltd. 
as Loaders of coal for the last ten years or more and that 
they have been woiking continuously and have put in attend¬ 
ance for 240 days oi more in a calendar year and that they 
have been doing the job under the control and supervision 
of the management The evidence on record also 
establishes the fact that while regular loadeis of the colliery 
have been getlmg Group HI wages as per N C W As, the 
concerned workmen have been getting Rs 20 per head per 
day provided each of them can load 4-1/2 tonnes of coal 
per day Admittedly, the main business of coal mines is 
to raise coal and to supply the same to the customers on 
consideration In order to supply coal to customers, loading 
of coal on truck or lailway wagons is required necessarily 
to be done In the context of facts and evidence on record. 

I come to the conclusion that the job of loading of coal 
IS permanent nature of job 

It has been alleged bv the union that the management has, 
Iieen disbursing wages to the concerned workmen through 
intermediaries below the lates as recommended bv the Wage 
Board Recommendations and N C W A I, II, III and IV 
The two concerned workmen, who have deposed before me, 
have stated emphatically that while the regular workmen of 
the colliery have been getting Wages as per N C W As , they 
have been gelling wages at the rate of Rs 20 for loading 
4-1 /2 tonnes coat per head per day , 

Anyway regard being bad to the evidence on record as^ 
aforesaid, I am constrained to hold that although the con¬ 
cerned workmen have been rendering services to the manage¬ 
ment of the colbeiy bv loading of coal fiom the colliery 
on trucks, the management has adopted the subterfuge of 
paying them wages through intermediary In the circums¬ 
tances, the concerned workmen shall be considered fo Be 


the woi kmen of pnncipal employer i e. management of the 
colliery and nci of the intermediary 

9 Shii D Mukherjee, authoiiscd repicsentative of tlie spon¬ 
soring union has submitted befoic me that the job of loading 
ot coal has been declared to be a prohibited category of job 
by the appropriate Goveinment by notification issued under 
the piovisions of Contract Labour (Regulation and Abolition) 
Act 1970 Both the concerned woikmen, S/Shn Gokul 
Ravvam and Nirm-al Kumar Chakrhborty have also asserted 
I hat loading of coal by engaging workmen through interme¬ 
diary IS piohibited Indeed, the position is really so The 
Central Goveinment issued a notification dated 1-2-1975 pro¬ 
hibiting employment of Contract Labour is five types of 
vvoik, such as, 0) Raising or raismg-cum-selllng of coal; (ii) 
Coal loading and unloading, (iii) Over burden removal and 
earjh cutting, (iv) Soft coke manufacturing and (v) Driving 
ot stone drifts and miscellaneous stone cutting underground 
It ippears ihal this notification was challenged before the 
Hon ble Patna High Court and Hoii’ble Court held that 
the notification issued was valid (1981 Bihar Revenue and 
labour Joiiinal—January Part-1 page 25) 

10 rhe management could not produce any document for 
registration of establishment Under the provisions of Con- 
tiHct Labour (Regulation and Abolition) Act, 1970 nor has 
the licence of the so-called intermediary for engaging work¬ 
men on the job of loading of coal been produced The pro- 
> istons contained m Sections 7 9 and 12 of the said Act 
envisage that (i) the establishment of the principal employer 
must be tegisteied and (u) the contractoi must have a vdid 
licence and if these conditions are not aatisfied then the 
Workmen even if employed by the itnermediary, shall bo 
cvinsideied to be the employees of the principal employer 1 e. 
the management (1990 Lab IC 1968 Gujarat High Court). 

11 Upon consideration of evidence on record and legal 
position I .im constiained to hold that the concerned work¬ 
men are leally the workmen of Kuiya colliery of M/s. 
BCC Ltd an I the management of the said colliery cannot 
shle away i(s obligation to absorb them in legular employ¬ 
ment and to pnv them Group-TII wages as per 'NCWA, IV. 

12 Now, the issue that comes to the fore of my consiJera- 
fion IS the back wages that the concerned workmen are 
entitled to gel fiom their employment The sponsoring union 
has tiaimed that the concerned workmen should bo paid 
back wages with ictrospective effect le with effect from the 
date of their tniployment The two concerned 'Workmen, who 
have deposed before me, have also claimed back wages 

It IS common knowledge that the coal Industry has Been 
passing thiough a critical phase It will bq hardly justified 
if the management is called upon to pay back wages to 
the coiiceined workmen from the date of their employment 
or fiom the dale when they made demand for their regula- 
iisation and pavment of proper wages on the management 
Taking a balanced view of the matter, I am of the opinion 
that the management of Kuiya Colliery shall pay Group-lH 
wages to the concemod workmen with effect from the date 
of Older of the Hon’ble High Court ic from 10-2-198B 
(bxt W 1) and should regularise the concerned workmen in 
service

as conditions precedent to retrenchment as laid

1947 have not been complied with. Being aggrieved

union raised an industrial dispute before the ALC

law. In compliance of the direction of the Hon’blc

Labour has referred the present dispute for adjudi¬ 
cation by this Tribunal. The management of Tisco, 
did not implement the Award of Ref. No. 28/78 
with good grace. The retrenchment in quick succes¬ 
sion is demonstration of grudge, ill will, malice 
and clearly indicative of victimisation and revenge¬ 
ful act. It has been asserted that the termination 
of the services of the concerned workman lanta- 
mounts to illegal retrenchment as conditions prece¬ 
dent to retrenchment as per Section 25-F and 25-N 
of the T. D. Act, 1947, have not been compiled 
with. The management applied to the Central

the provisions of the I, D. Act. In the circums¬ 
tances. the union has prayed that the Award be 
passed holding Illegal termination of the services 
of the concerned workman illegal with a direction 
upon the management to reinstate the concerned 
workman in service with payment of full back wages. 

4. In the rejoinder to the W.S. of the sponsoring union 
the management has denied and disputed the facts with 
regard to the Award of this Tribunal passed in Ref. No. 
28/78 and submitted that the General Manager (Collieries) 
who was superior to the Agent of the Colliery was compe¬ 
tent to terminate the services of the concerned workman. 
Shri G. Prasad did not sign the order Of terijiination of the 
services of the concerned workman. The concerned work¬ 
man was paid 3 months notice pay and back wages before 
14-8-80 when the termination order came into eflect. It is 
wrong to suggest that the Award was not implemented in 
good grace. In view of the .Award passed in Ref. No. 28/78 
there was no necessity for obtaining fresh permission from 
the Central Government and a.s .a measure of abundant pre¬ 
caution the management took permis,sion. Anyway the pro¬ 
vision relating to the approval of retrenchment has been 
declared ultra vires by the Madras High Court. The Central 
Government, after going through the Award and other rele¬ 
vant documents, held the action of the management justified 
and impliedly granted permission for retrenchment. The 
pjc.sent reference is due to the direction of the Hon’ble High 
Court in order to give a chance to the concerned workman 
to present his case before the Tribimal. All legal formalities 
relating to retrenchment were complied with fermo nf 
‘h- Award. 

5. In rejoinder to the W.S. of the management the spon¬ 
soring union has reiterated the fact as contained in his W.S. 
and emphasized that the termination of the services of the

6 . TOc jnanagement. in order to justify its action has

7. Admittedly Shn Ruinayan Thakur, llie concerned work¬

The concerned workman raised an industrial dispute over the

Ref. No. 28/78. The dispute was adjudicated by Hon'blo 
Mr. Justice B. K. Ruy who was then the Presiding Officer of 
the Tribunal. A copy of the Av.'ard has been produced by 
the management and marked as Ext. M-1. It appears from 
the Award that Mr. Justice Ray. upon consideration of 
evidence on record came to the following factual con¬ 
clusion :— 

“Th* evidence led by the management fully est.iblishes 
its case that the workman concerned wrote three 
.anonymous letters Ext, M-4. M-5 and M-6 threaten¬ 
ing Dr. Chawla" and the members of his family. 
I have also no hesitation in mind to hold that the 
workman himself admitted that he was the author 
of those letters and he m.adc endoisemcnt Exts. M-1, 
M-2 and M-3 in his ov/n hand and under his own 
signatures in those letters. It is not disputed that 
the workman concerned belongs to the Security 
department and us such he is to protect life of the 
emnloveps of the company and go guard its propertv 
That being so, when a personnel in a security 
denartmenf of the company himself threatens other 
emnlovees of the c^nipanv as found in Exts. M-4. 
M-5 and M-6 he is hound tn loose confidence and 
trust of the management But merely because an 
order of termination of service of a workman on 
the face of it appear-' tn be an order of termination 
simnliciler. still the Tribtiinl has to cn behind the 
order tn find out If the order is (he result of bonu- 
fide exercise of newer vested In tbe numaeement or 
is a mere cloak to cover a case of victimisation. 
Mr. Mukhertre concedes tn this noeition of law 
hut submits that if the evidence ted hv fh^ manage¬ 
ment is accepted, thou* >’s nothing to show that 
the order of termination in Que.stion is not a hona- 
flde exercise of power bv tf’c maungement or is the 
result of victimisation TTi,'^ contention of Mr. 
Mnkheriee has sulTIctent fo'ce. 1 have alrpaUv said 
that evidence of rhetwo witnesses examined hv the 
management is trustworthy and on the basts of 
that evidence ft most he held that the order of ter¬ 
mination Is bons fide one and there is nothing to 
chow ttist if it tI-, rc'iilr of victimts'.ifion.” 
nt-en again Mr. Justice R.'y v.aa pleased to hold ns 
follows :—■ 

“In the case of Delhi Cloth and General Mills Co. 
Ltd. V. Shambhii Nath Mukherjee (AIR 1978 SC 
8 ) it was held that striking off the names of the 
workman from the rolls amoiim to retrenchment 
under Section 2(oo) of the Act. It was also held 
in that case that whenever the employer purports 
to terminate the services of an employee, which 
amounts to be retrenchment within the meaning 
of the Act the same has to he in conformity with 
the lequircments of Section ,2.5-F. If there is non- 
compliance, tbe order of termination shall be vitia¬ 
ted. This being the position of law I hold that 
the order of termination in the ease before me 
amounts to an order of rctrcndirac-nt and the pro¬ 
visions of Section 25-T- not having been complied 
with the order cannot he sustained in law. The 
workman concerned is, Iherefoie entitled to icin- 
'tatement with all hack wages from the date of 
termination (ill the date of his reinstatement. 

T have alreadv held (hat the inirmafieil order of te<mi- 
nation of services of the workman concerned is in 
bonafide exercise of powers bv the management 





w ^rsi'Tsr : ’rr^ 9,i99i/we5rr^ is^ i9i? 


1225 


reserved to it is parayiuphs 13 and 14 ol the Stand¬ 
ing Orders Ext. M-B, that the provisions in para¬ 
graphs 13 and 14 ot E.xt. M-13 are valid and en- 
torceablc that the workinaji who belonged to the 
ScQunty Department of the management acted in 
a manner which resulted ni lo.ss ot conhdcnce in 
him by the management and that after loss of con¬ 
fidence in the workman it was open to the manage¬ 
ment to employment of the workman by an order 
of termination simplieiter. But as in mow of the 
established position oi hnv already indicated the 
order of termination of the services of the work¬ 
man has been found to be an order of retrench¬ 
ment and so the order has been held to be not 
sustainable, the provisions of Section 25-E of I. D. 
Act not having been complied with, it is open to 
the management once again in take recourse to its 
power in paragraphs 13 and 14 of Ext. M-13 and 
to pass an order of termination in accordance with 
law if it so likes. The rcfcrqnce is answered 
accordingly.” 

y. The net fall-out ol the Awaid passed by Mr. Justice 
Ray IS that since the management did not comply with the 
provision of Section 2i-F of the Act, the. order of tci/nina- 
tion of the services of the concerned workman which was 
found to be an order of retrenchment was not sustainable 
and it was Open to the management onCe again to take 
recourse to its power in paragraph.s 13 and 14 of Certified 
Standing Orders and to pa's an order of termination in 
accordance with law. if it so liked. 

9. It appears that the appcopiialc Government initially

1(1 It i‘- the firm case of the management that the Award 
of this Tribunal w'lis implemented and the concerned work¬ 
man was reinstalcd in service with effect fiom 1-2-77 but 
ugnin ihe management terminated his services with eft'ect 
from 14 fi-80 and paid him retrenchment compensation, notice 
pay and back wages and other dues. 

The case ot the .sponsoring union is that the management 
did not implement the Award in good grace. Anyway the 
union has contended that one Shri G. Prasad again termi¬ 
nated the services of the concerned workman by letter dated 
1-8-KO with effect fiom 14-8-80 and that Shri Prasad had no 
legal authority to 'erminato the services of the cocnerned 
workman. It has also been contended by the union that the 
termination of (ho services of the concerned workman . mounts 
to illegal retrenchment and malafide action of the manage¬ 
ment ns the conditio.ns precedent to retrenchment ns laid 
down in Section 25F and 25M of the I.D. Act, 1947 have 
not been complied with. 

II, From (he pleadings of the parties it is evident that 
the management implemented the Award passed in Ref. 
No. 28/78. In otherwise, the concerned workman was re¬ 
instated in service with all back wages from the dale of 
teim'nalion till the date of his reinstatement. 

It is an undeniable position that the sepices of the con¬ 
cerned workman are again terminated with effect from 
14-8-80 on the ground of loss of confidence in him by the 
mangement. The letter of the General Manager (Collieries) 
dated 8-8-80 addressed to the concerned workman reads

"Peg.—Awaid dated 18th March, 1990 of the Central

It appears from the evidence of MW-1 S. N. Sinha, Chief 
Personnel Mana^r in Tisco. collieries at Jamadoba that the 
management paid all ihc dues to the concerned workman 
before terminating his service.', such as back wages, rotionch- 
ment compensation. Bonus and notice pay. He has proved 
the payment register of the establishment which shows (hat a

in settlement of'all his dues Ext, M-8. The management pic- 
parod voucher fur the amount Hxt. M-9 and prepared a cheque 
for the ameunt Ext. M-14 and produced Banker's statement 
E.xt. M-lf) to show thid the cheque was encashed by the 
concerned workman. Besides it appears that the management 
paid him one months viagcs in lieu of notice through re¬ 
mittance by M.O. The voucher for M.O. remittance ha© been 
marked Ext. M-12. Tbus from the evidence on record it is 
established that the concerned workman, upon reinstatement 
was paid back wages t.nd on termination of his sci'vicM, 
■was paid compensation and one months pay in lieu of naJice 
ns envisaged in Section 25F of the I.D. i^t., 1947 Besides, 
be was paid 2 tnonths pay in lieu of notice prasuinably in 
compliance with the piovision of section 25N of the f.D, Act, 
1947 as obtaining in (980. The concerned workman has not 
come forward before me to deny and dispute that he did not 
receive payment of back wages and compensation for re¬ 
trenchment and one r.-enths pay in lieu of notice from the 
management. 

U appears that a noti'-t was sent to the appropriate Oovern- 
irent by tlie management for approval of terminaden of 
services of the concerned workman Ext. M~4, But the appro¬ 
priate Government declined to accord approavl on tbe ground 
that there was no fovision for obtaining post-facto permis¬ 
sion for retrenchment under Section 25N of the I.D. Act, 
Ext. W-1, The provision of Section 25N of the I.D, Act, as 
obtaining in 1980 was struck down by a Division Beech of 
the Madras High Court in a ease reported in 1981 Lab. 
T.C. 799 (K. V. Rajendran-vi'-Dv Commissioner of Labour 
Madurai) and also by Rajasthan High Court in a case reported 
in 1984 Lab. I.C. NCC 40 (J.. K. Synthetics-vrs-Uruen of 
India). This being the po_sition provision of Section 25F of 
the I.D. Act. 1947 us obtaining in 1980 shall be considered 
to bo non oxistant and so the refusal of the appropriate Gov- 
emment to accord pein’ission for retrenchment Is considered 
otiose. In the circumstances tbe provision of Section 2iF of 
the ID. Act, 1947 should hold the field. The mapageinent 
has complied with the piovision of Section 25F of Ihe I.D 
Act before resorting to retrenchment of the concemctt 
workman, 

12. Shri B. N. .fihaiiT.a, authorised represented <f the 
concerned workman has .submitted that in the case of 
termination of .services on the ground of loss of confiaenee 
holding of domestic enquiry is necessary as loss of coDfldenco 
casts stigma. In support of his contention he has referred 
to ine a decision of the Hon’ble Supreme Court reported in 
1985 ELI Vol, 1 (Chandulal-vrs-Management of Pan American 
Woild Airways Inc.) page 181. The decision lays down the

proposition that want of confidence in an employee does point

mvoivmg loss ot connoencc. in any viCw ot the matter Ihis

conclusion that the action of the management of M/s. Titco.

“The action of the management of Tisco. Ltd.’s Sijna, 
Colliery in terminating the services of Shri Ramayan 
Thakur, Watchman is justifled and consequently the 
concerned workmaa is entiled to no lellef,” 

In the circumslanccs of th.t case T awaid no costs.

2. Shn S G Diddi (hereafter ‘the Workman’) was

3, The Workman's case is that the Enquiry Officer 
held him guilty of the charge, simply on the basis of 
his alleged confession in a letter dated 17th February, 
1986 addressed to the Management. According to 
him a confess|ion by itself cannot form valid basis of 
a finding of guilt, and the Management should have 
led! evidence of material witnesses and alp duly 
j^roved the documents on which they relied. He 
challenges the Disiplinary Authorities Order of dis¬ 
missal dated 7th October, 1987 and the Order of 
Appellate Authority dated 2fth Febniary, 1988 con¬ 
firming the penalty on the aforesaid identical 
ground. His next submission is that in py case the 
penally i'- disproportionately harsh, considering tfis 
unblemished past record and the fact that he made 
good the amount of Rs, 13000 voluntarilly, even before 


the fraud (the Workman refers to it as 'irregularity') 
was deducted by the Management, He now seeks 
reinstatement with full back wages. 

4. The Management resist the Workman’s claim 
in toto. According to them, the Workman had con¬ 
fessed to his guilt not only in' his letter dated 17th 
February, 1986 before the domestic enquiry was 
initiated, but also during the course of it on diverse 
occasions before the Enquiry Officer, the Disciplinary 
Authority and finally the Appellate Authority. The 
Management urge that there was nothing improper, 
much less illegal, in basing the finding of guilt on the 
confession and justify award of the extreme penalty, 
in view of the gjavity of the circumstances of the 
case. 

5. In his rejoinder, the Workman refers to the case 
of an Officer of the Bank, Shri Beliga, where far 
lenient punisliment of withholding two increments 
only was awarded, although the charges proved aga’ints 
him were far more serious. According to him, the 
Management being ‘State’ w'ithia the meaning of 
Article 12 of the Constitution, is not entitled to dis¬ 
criminate him vis-a-vis an Officer. 

6. The points for determination and my findings 
thereon are given below : 

Points for determination Finding 

1. Is the finding of guilt bad 

for the reason that it is 

based merely on the confe.s No. It is 

sion of the Workman 1 good. 

Sec last para 
for the award. 
Sec the last 
para. 

2. Does the penalty awarded

3. What reliefs if any, is the 

Workman entitled to 7 

REASONS FOR FINDINGS 

7. The parties have not led any oral evidence. By 
consent, documents filed by them have been exhi¬ 
bited. I have also heard the representatives of the 
two sides—Srinivasa Babu for the Workman and 
Shri Kukkilaya for the Management. 

8. Although the Workman has challenged the 
correctness of the fin'ding of the guilt and the quan¬ 
tum of punishment, he is not challenging the validity 
and fairness of the enquiry. As such, 1 am proceed¬ 
ing straight to the merits. The main' challenge to 
the finding of guilt is that it is based on the Work¬ 
man’s confession alone, which, accerding to him, is 
not permissible ini Law. The second submission is 
that the Enquiry Officer should not have permitted 
the Management’s representative to cross-examine the 
Workman, when the latter had not- tendered himself 
as his witness. It is also urged tlial the Management 
.should have examined the Cashier un,d Customer Kale 




1229 


[vrrq II—qj . iijj -f-r ^ 1^74 jtrt' t>,i99i/Tr?7^7 18,1 ok 


in respect of whole Savings Account, that the work¬ 
man had committed the fraud. On consideration of 
the facts and circumstances of the case as a whole. 
1 find no substance in any of these submissions. 

9. There is no rule of Law or prudence against 
basing a finding of guilt in a domestic enquiry on the 
admission of the Workman alone, if the admission is 
voluntary. In the present case there arc more than 
adequate materials on the record to''uphold the ad¬ 
missions as absolutely voluntary. He admitted his 
guilt not only in his letter of 17-2-86, but also on 
atleast five subsequent occasions. Ex. M-3 is the letter 
dated 11-6-86 written by him to the Management in 
reply to the chargesheet. Ex. M-5 are the proceed¬ 
ings of the Ifomestic Enquiry held on 18-2-87. On 
both these occasions the workmen admitted his guilt 
in all d^etails and prayed for mercy on the ground 
that in a moment of weakness he had succumbed to 
the temptation of collecting money or paying off 
dowery to the parents of his would be son-in-law. 
Ex. M-8 is a notice by the Asstt. General Manager 
(Disciplinary Authority) to the Workman to show-’ 
cause against the proposed penalty of dismissal. Ex. 
M-9 is the Workman’s reply dated 22nd August, 
1987. Ex. M-11 is his letter 16th November, 1987 
to the General Manager, purporting to be the memo¬ 
randum of appeal. Ex. M-12 are the proceedings 1 
appeal, dated 17-2-88. On all these occasions the 
workman again admitted his guilt unequivocally and 
only prayed for mercy. Even here he has not retract¬ 
ed from his admissions. I am fully satisfied that the 
admissions are voluntary and true and that the En¬ 
quiry Officer did not go wrong in accepting and act¬ 
ing upon them. 

10. It is true that the Enquiry Officer permitted 
the Management’s representative to put some ques¬ 
tions to the workman and exhibited the documents 
on the strength of his admissions. The Management 
also did not examine Customer Kale in resnect of 
whose account the Workman committed the fraud 
and Cashier Gadad who made two payments to him. 
However, I am satisfied that those omissions have 
not caused any prejudice to the workman on merits. 
The questions put by the Management’s representative 
to the workman related to the authenticity of various 
Bank documents connected witK tlie incident. The 
workman candidly admitted those documents and 
also gave full details of the circumstances in which he 
fell a prey to the termptation of encashing the two 
''beques. Indeed, eyen left to himself, he would have 
been required to reveal most of these circumstances, 
in order to persuade the D'scipVnarv Authortv to 
take a lenient view In any case he had alreadv ad¬ 
mitted his guilt at least on t'vir earlier occasions 
namelv in his Tetters dated 17-2-86 and 11-6-86 (Fv. 
M-2 & Ex M-3) before lie made his statement in 
the domestic enquiry on 18-2-87 These was no 
purpose in examining the Customer and the Cash’er 
also in the circumstances of the case. There is no 
substance in the workman’s submissions. 

11. The Enquiry Officer, Disciplinary Authority 
and Appellate Authority were absolutely right in ac¬ 
cepting and acting upon the confession of the work¬ 
man and finding him guilty on its basis. I answer issue 
no. 1 accordin^y. 


12. Point No. 2 ; The allegations imputed to the 

workman and voluntarily admitted by him are no 
doubt grave. Normally dismissal would be quite justi¬ 
fied in a case of defalcation. However m the present 
case it appears that the workman was caught in the 
meshes of a difficult situation, which was not an ex¬ 
clusive creation of his own. This is what he stated 
before the Enquiry Officer on 18-2-1987 - 

“On 4th December, 1985 my eldest daughter’s 
marriage engagement has taken place. As 
per old custom we have to arrange for the 
payment of entire dowry amount which was 
agreed earlier should be paid on the day of 
engagement only. Since I was not having 
the dowry money I requested bridegroom 
people to give me one month time to ar¬ 
range for the same, for which they agreed. 
But to my badluck I could not secure the 
necessaiy required amount to pay, as agre¬ 
ed earlier. Then again I have requested the 
bridegroom people to give me some more 
time to arrange for the payments. Then 
they have given the ultimatum to pay the 
amoun within 3-4 days and they win break 
the engagement. All these non-availability of 
the fund and my financial difficulties came 
to be known by my daughter and she also 
started crying by saying all these troubles 
were due to her marriage and if she is not

14, Before parting with the case, 1 may briefly 
refer to the submission of the workman with regard 
to BaUga. According to him, because this Ollicer was 
treated more leniently by the Management, he also 
deserves to be similarly treated. For this submissions 
he presses in aid, the fundamental right of equality 
enshrined in Article 14 of the Constitution. 1 must 
record my disapproval of this approach. I may make 
it clear that Baliga is not involved in the present 
fraud. Assuming that he had committed misconduct|s 
of equal or even grosser gravity. I do not think that 
will afford legitimate justification for the present 
workman to claim for himself undeserved mercy. If 
his plea was accepted, that would be tentamount to 
perpetrating one more wrong in order to case the 
judicial conscience by the pretence that thereby the 
earlier wrong stands mitigated. If the Management 
have filtered in their duty to mete out just and ade¬ 
quate punishment to Baliga—this is not my onsider- 
ed or even provisional impression—it is for the higher 
Authorities to see that could be done to right the mat¬ 
ter, So far as their reference is concerned. I am clear 
that Article 14 or any other legitimate factor does 
not come in my way in doing full and complete jus¬ 
tice in the case. On its own merits, without being in- 
flaenced by the facts of Baliga’s case. That is all.

REASONS 

ISSUE NO. 1 

6 . The lady Mrs, K, J, Mendjoge filed her affidavit 
at E\. 9 in support of her case. She was cros,s- 
examined on behalf of the Bank management. Shri 
U. B. Rairikar, the Manager (P) of the Bank, Hied 
his affidavit at Ex. 11 in support of the contentions 
of the Bank management and he was cross-examined 
on behalf of the said lady. The material docu¬ 
mentary evidence on record is thus :— 

By the letter dated 12-9-19S4 (Ex. .*i) the 
Superintendent of the Bank had informed 
the lady Mrs. .Toshi that as she bad ex¬ 
pressed her inability to accept the offer of 
higher as.signment, she is deemed to have 
declined it, and as such .she is barred for 
such higher assignment ncrmanentlv as per 
. the promotion nolicy. By the letter dated 
29-6-1985 (Ex. 6) the Regional Manager of 
the Bank informed the said ladv Mrs.'^Joshi 
that the Bank was pleaded to offer the post 
of Current Account Machine Operator at 
Pune City Branch to her with effect from 
1-7-1985 an'd she should communicate her 
willingness immediately. Accordingly Mrs., 
.loshi accepted that post. By the letter 
dated 26-5-1987 (Ex. 71 the Regional 
Manager again informed Mrs. .Toshi 
that the previous letter' of the Bank dated 
29-6-1985 designating her as Accounts 


Machine Operator is hereby treated as con- 
celled, as she is barred for any higher as¬ 
signment upto 21-5-1987, as such she .should 
cease to draw the special allowance of 
Rs- 216 with immediate effect. Thereafter 
the Regional Manager by the letter dated 
26-5-1987 (Ex. 8) informed the lady in 
question, namely Mrs. Mendjoge, that she 
is designated as Accounts Machine Operator 
at Pune City Branch with immediate effect 
and she should convey her willingness im¬ 
mediately, Tt was further mentioned in that 
letter that as long as she was required to per- 
foim the duties of Machine Operator, she 
would be entitled to a Special Allowance of 
Rs. 216 per month. Therefore, according to 
the Bank management, the said post of 
Accounts Machine Operator assigned to Mrs. 
Mendjoge was not a permanent post and 
that she was to get the Special Allowance 
as long as she was required to perform the 
duties of Machine Operator. By the letter 
dated 8-10-1987 (Ex. 131 the Regional 
Manager of the Bank again informed Mrs, 
Mendjoge that the letter dated 26-5-1987 
designating her a.s Accounts Machine Ope¬ 
rator is hereby treated as concelled and 
she will cease to draw the special allowance 
with immediate effect. 

7. Therefore, the admitted facts are that the post 
of Accounts Machine Operator was firstly assigned 
to Mrs. Joshi, that it was then withdrawn from her 
and it was assigned to Mrs Mendjoge. and that it 
was withdrawn from Mrs Mendjoge, and was reas¬ 
signed to Mrs. Joshi. According to the Bank mana¬ 
gement. the post of Accounts Machine Operator which 
was firstly assigned to Mr.s. Joshi was later on with¬ 
drew from her onlv through mistake. According *o 
Bank m.nnaeement. Mrs. .Toshi had refused the assign¬ 
ment of the post o.f Special Assistant, and not of the 
post of Accounts Machine Operator, and as such 410 
wa<^ in fact entitled to the post of Accounts Machine 
Operator, and onlv through mistake it was with¬ 
drawn from her. Further, according to the Bank 
management, the post of Accounts Machine Ope- 
lafor. after withdrawing it from Mrs. Joshi was 
offered to Mrs. Mendjoge, that Mrs. Mendjoge 
was not entitled and eligible to that po‘t 
.and hence later on that post was withdrawn 
from her. In the present leferenee, we are onlv 
concerned with the assignment of the post of Accoimt; 
Machine Operator to Mrs Mendioee and its with¬ 
drawal from her. T find that while withdrawing the 
post of Accounts Machine Operator from Mrs, Mend¬ 
ioee the Bank management had not complied with the 
provisions of Section 9A of the Industrial Disputes 
Act. 

8. As per Section 9A of the Industrial Disputes 
Act. no employer, who proposes to effect anv change 
ia the conditions of service annlieahle to anv work¬ 
man in respect of anv matter specified in the Fourth 
Schedule, shall effect such change, (a) without giving 
to the workman likelv to he affected by such change 
a notice 'in the prescribed manner of the nature of the 
cf'apco proposed to be effected or (h) within twenty- 
one dnvr of giving snrh notice: provided that no 
notice shall be required for effecting any such change 




1233 


%rr7fr w ^T^iTir s.i'jsi/'^rsiT^' i«,i9i2 


[■rr, j(ii)J 

(c) where the change is efi'ected in pursuance of any 

settlement or award; or.As per the items 

mentioned in the 'Fourth Sehedule compensatory and 
other allowances relate to the conditions of service, 
ior the change of which notice is' to be given. In the 
piesent ease the post of Accounts Machine Operator 
carries a spec'al allowance of Rs. 216 per month. Asj 
such, the assignment of that post relates to the con-, 
ditjons O'! service. After the said post of Accounts 
Machine Operator was assigned to Mrs. Mendjogc, 
the Bank Management thought of withdrawing that 
post by the letter dated 8-10-87 (Ex- 13). However no 
notice as required and contemplated under Section 
yA of the Industrial Disputes Act, was given by the 
Bank management to Mrs, Mendjogc before the said 
post was withdrawn from her. According to the Bank 
management, when the post of Accounts Machine 
Operator was offered to Mrs- Mendjogc, she was in 
fact was not entitled and was not eligible to it, and 
It was wrongly assigned to her. Even then, in my 
opinion, notice as required under Section 9-A of the 
Industrial Disputes Act should have been given by 
the Bank management before the necessary orders 
were passed. The Bank management had assigned the 
said post to Mrs. Mendjoge under the belief that she 
was eligible for the post and hence that post was 
assigned to her. According (o the Bank management, 
through mistake that post was offered and assigned 
to Mrs. Mendjogc, and that mistake was later on recti¬ 
fied, and that post was withdrawn from her. How¬ 
ever, as the said post was firstly olTcied to her on the 
ground that, accoiding to the management, she \Vas 
then eligible to that post. Therefore, the notice under 
vSection y-A of the fndustrial Disputes Act was neces¬ 
sary. The Bank management should have informed 
her, and she should have been heard in the matter 
to her and that it was proposed to he withdraw,! from 
her, and she should have been hcaid in' the matter 
and then only the necessary orders should have been 
passed. However, this procedure has not been followed 
by the management before withdrawing that post 
from Mrs. Mendjoge. Further, the Bank witness 
Shri Rairikar admitted in his cross-'ximinatjon that 
the posts of Accounts Machine Operator! were filled 
in on permanent basis as per station-wise seniority, 
and that the post of Accounts Maclnne Operator was 
offered to Mrs. Mendjoge Station-wise sen'oijiy, and 
therefore that post was a permanent post, and not a 
temporary post, as urged by the Bank management. 
Thercfre, a notice under ‘Section 9-A of the Indus¬ 
trial Disputes Act was absolutely neec.ssary before the 
order regarding withdrawal of that post was passed 
against her, 1 find that the Bank managemeut had com¬ 
mitted a breach of the provisions contained in Section 
9-A of the Industrial Disputes Act in withdrawing the 
said post fiom Mrs. Mendjogc. Issue No. 1 is foiin-l 
in the affirmative. 


ISSUES NOS. 2 AND 3 

As the Bank management, while withdrawing 
the post of Accounts Machine Operator from Mrs, K. 
J. Mendjoge did not comply with provisions of 
Section 9-A of the Industrial Disputes .Act, the action of 
the management in withdrawing the higher allowance 
post of Accounts Machine Operator from Mrs. Mend¬ 
joge is not just, valid an'd legal. Issue No, 2 is found 
in the negative. The relief, therefore, the employee 
Mrs. K, J. Mendjoge is ent;itled to receive the spe¬ 
cial Allowance of the post of Accounts Machine Ope¬ 
rator from the date it was withdrawn from her till 
the date it was again assigii'cd to her. Issue No, 3 is 
found accordingly. 

ISSUE NO. 4 

10. The following Award is therefore passed. 
AWARD 

(i) The action of the management of Union 
Bank of India in withdrawing the higher 
allowance post of Machine Operator assigned 
to Mis. K. J. Mendjoge is not just, valid 
and legal. 

(ii) The management is hereby directed to pay 
the necessary allowance of the post of Ac¬ 
counts Machine Operator to Mrs. K. J. 
Mendjoge from the date it was withdrawn 
from her till the date it was again assigned 
to her. 

(iii) The parties to bear their own costs of this 
Reference.

and documents from the godown, and carrying of 
papers, records and documents from one table to

8. Point No. 3 : 1 wiU clear a short but vital point 
before 1 proceed to decide whether the Workman had 
worked for more than 240 days in the 12 months per- 
ceding the termination of his service. It is with le- 
gard to the date of termination. In the Statement of 
Clanm, this date is expressly stated to be 15-12-84. 
The Management have not denied this fact in their 
written statement. Now at the evidence stage they 
have aken the stand that the date of termination was 
3-5-85. Obviously the Management cannot be allow¬ 
ed to make such a vital shift on an admitted position. 
It is true that the Workman was again engaged on 
26-1-85 and he continued to work with two breaks till 
2-5-85, while correspondence was going on between 
the Management and their Mctropiolitan Head Office 
at Bombay, regarding his absorption in the rcgiila- 
cadre. Tlic proposal was ultimately not okayed by the 
Bombay Office and Management did not retain tlie 
Workman aflci 2-5-85, However, these circuni'-tan- 
ccs do not detract from the admitted stand in the 
pleadings that the cnicia) date of termination is 
15-12-84. For computing the number of days for 
which the Workman had actually worked, 12 Calender 
months prcced'iig this date will be material, I am 
proceeding further on this basis. 

9. The Workman affirms in hii affidavit ibat he had 
worked for 253 days during the period 30-1-S4 to 
15-12-S4. He claims to have reached this figure after 
verifying the vouchers. No direct suggestion is made 
in tlie cross-examination that bis calculations are 
wrong. Only a general question is put to him tvhelher 
he had copies of the vouchers with hini. The Work¬ 
man replied in the negative. Obviously the vouchers 
would be in the custody of the Management and not 
the Workman. 

10. In rebuttal, Bhandarkar has given a tally of the 
Workman’s actually working days in paras 7 to 11 of 
his afh,davit. The details are abeady extracted in 
para 6 supra. If Bhnndarkair’s evidence is believ.^d at 
its face value the tally for the period 30-1-84 to 
15-12-84 comes to 235 days. It is not explained why 


tile Management did not produce the record that was 
available with them on this vital question, partieiilai- 
ly wlien Bhandarkar affirms to 1 a\e bused liii affida¬ 
vit on it. 1 may also add that even otherwise 1 am not 
much inspiicd by the infallibility of the Management's 
record. In Oieir letter dated 3u-12-85 fLx. M-l) ad- 
Uresaed to their Chief Manager, the Management ad¬ 
mit that the details given by them m their earner 
letter ol 21 11-85 about the number ol tlays on wliich 
the Workman had worked, were wnrng and infact he 
httd worked for 10 days more. All the same, the fair 
and prudent course for the Management would have 
been to produce the record whatever worth it was be- 
tere the Iribunal, This was not done. The eros.-)- 
exarnination of the Workman is also casual. In 1982 
1 LLJ 33 Workmen of MjS. Will’amscn Magor and 
Company Limited and MiS. William Magor ik Com¬ 
pany Limited and another. The Supreme Couit has 
ruled that where there is reasonable doubt on faco. 
it should go to the weaker sections of the so¬ 
ciety. For all these reasons, I would prefer tile testi¬ 
mony of the Workman to that of Dhandarkar and 
hold that the former had actually worked tor 253 
days upto 15-12-1984. 

11. There is one more reason in support of the 
Workman’s case. A look at the data reproduced in 
para 6 supra, will show that the Workman has prac¬ 
tically v/orked lor 40 full weeks, inclusive of Sundays, 
Admittedly Sundays are oft days tor the Bank m ques¬ 
tion, Noinially the Management would have welcom¬ 
ed him to work on these days also, haa they not been 
declared compulsorily closed days under section iS 
of the Bombay shops and Establislu.ieius Act. In their 
wrilicn subm ssions the Managemem claim ih.it tlie 
provision ot the aforesaid Act will not apply to a 
Nationalised Bank, which the Mancgcment adriiiled- 
ly are. [fhey rely on a ruling of the Madras High 
Court icportcd in 55 company cases 358 Union Bunk 
of India Vs, Additional Commis,s'oner for Workmti 's 
Compensation. This decision in lact supports the 
Workman. The question for decision before the learn¬ 
ed Single Judge was whether the exemption granted 
under section 4(l)(c) of the Jamil Nadu Shops and 
Establi.shments Act to “Establishments under the Cen¬ 
tral Government", applied to Nationrdi.sed Banks also. 
The learned Judge underlined tiie diflcrence betvve'u 
‘an Establishment under the Central Government ‘and' 
an Establushment of the Cential Government and 
held that Nationalised Breaks fell under the former 
calcgory and not the latter and us such they were 
entitled to exemption. Under the Bombay Shops and 
Establishments Act, exemption is granted to the esta- 
blislimento of '.he Central Government. So the cxemp- 
lion from compulsory weekly pej holidays will not 
apply in Mahaiashtra to Nationalised Banks, as they 
are not establishments of the Central Government 
Thus tlie Management were unUr section 18 of the 
BS.E. Act, oVi'oged to ^ivc on 3 paid holiday to tlie 
Workman after extracting work from him for 6 
days. If due consideration is given to this aspect the 
number of wo-king days for the Workman upto 15th 
December 1984 rises to 275, even on the assiinq o, n 
that I'c had ucu,ally worked for 235 days only. X' 
claimed by Bhandarkar. Viewed from any angle I am 
satisfied that the Workman had acTadly worked lor 
more than 240 days during (h - cruei.tl period ,4' ' 2 
months preceding 15-12-84. 1 return an allrmutive 
finding on ibis point.

bunal passed the following. 

AWARD 

This dispute between the workmen and the mariagc- 
ment of the Food Corporation of India, Madra, 
arises out of a reference under Section 1(1(1)(d) ot 
the industiial Disputes Act, J947 by the Govein- 
ment of India in its Order No. L-42012(37) I83.D II 
(BtlD.lV.BjD.V. dated 3-4-1984 of the Ministry ol 
Labour & Rehabilitation for adjudication of the 
following issue :— 

“Whether tlie action of the management of the 
Food Corporation o*^ India, Madras in ter¬ 
minating the services of Shn Adaikkan, 
NMR workman at their Avadi Depot Madras 
(Construction and maintenance) w.c.f. 
28-9-76 and not absorbing him as messen¬ 
ger (Depot) on regular basi.s alongwith hi.s 
juniors wc.f. 22-3-1979 is justified—If not, 
to what relief he is entitled to.” 

2. I’arlics were served with summons. 

3. The Petitioner-workman Thiru Adaikkan tiled 
his claim statement on 14-5-1986 putting forth his 
claim. The respondent management was absent and 
not lepresented and set ex-parte. No counter stale- 
nicnt was filed. The petitioner was examined as W.W, 

I and an award was passed on 22-8-1986 holding 
the termination of the petitioner as illegal and direct¬ 
ing h's reinstatement with back wages, continuity of 
service and other attendant benefits. 

4. On 29-8-1986, the respondent management filed 
a Miscellaneous Application No. 9411986 with coun¬ 
ter statement praying to set aside the exparte award 
passed on 22-8-15)86. On 2-2-1987 this Tribunal has 
passed an order setting aside the ex parte odder and 
allowing the applidation.

appearin W.A. No. 40311988 before the High Court,

S.O. 732.—In pursuance of sub-section (iij ol 
Clause (g) of section 2 of the Iron Ore Mines,

published in Part II, Section 3, Sub-section (ii) of the

cified in the schedule below to be metallurgical fac¬

Mines Labour Welfare Cess Act, 1976 (55 of 1976),
